=== PROMPT VARIANT ===
r1

=== PROMPT ===
You will be given a competitive programming problem.
There are N people divided into three teams.
The people are numbered 1, 2, \ldots, N, and the teams are numbered 1, 2, 3. Currently, person i belongs to team A_i.
Each person has a value called strength; person i has a strength of B_i. The strength of a team is defined as the sum of the strengths of its members.
Determine whether it is possible for zero or more people to switch teams so that all teams have equal strength. If it is possible, find the minimum number of people who need to switch teams to achieve this.
You cannot create new teams other than teams 1, 2, 3.

Input

The input is given from Standard Input in the following format:
N
A_1 B_1
A_2 B_2
\vdots
A_N B_N

Output

If it is possible to make all teams have equal strength, print the minimum number of people who need to switch teams. Otherwise, print -1.

Constraints


- 3 \leq N \leq 100
- A_i \in \lbrace 1, 2, 3 \rbrace
- For each x \in \lbrace 1, 2, 3 \rbrace, there exists some i with A_i = x.
- 1 \leq B_i
- \displaystyle\sum_{i = 1}^{N} B_i \leq 1500 
- All input values are integers.

Sample Input 1

6
1 2
2 5
1 5
3 3
1 3
3 6

Sample Output 1

2

If person 1 switches to team 3 and person 4 switches to team 2, all teams will have a strength of 8.

Sample Input 2

4
1 1
1 2
2 3
3 4

Sample Output 2

-1

Sample Input 3

3
1 1
2 1
3 1

Sample Output 3

0

Sample Input 4

12
2 5
1 4
3 3
2 3
3 9
1 2
2 2
3 9
2 6
1 9
1 1
3 1

Sample Output 4

3


Reason in a commit-implement-recheck way.

A productive rhythm for this task usually looks like this:
- Isolate the decisive constraints, the target complexity, and at most one or two approaches that are still truly plausible.
- If two candidates remain, compare them only on the decisive constraint and commit quickly to the stronger one instead of keeping a wide search open.
- Before coding, keep only a tiny implementation ledger for the chosen plan: main invariant, key state or transition, and the one or two edge cases most likely to fail.
- Move into code as soon as that ledger is clear. After coding starts, keep reasoning attached to the current draft rather than reopening the high-level search.
- After the first complete draft, do one short implementation-check-adjust-check loop on the same draft: test the key edge case, patch locally if needed, and recheck.
- Reopen the main design only if a concrete counterexample breaks the core invariant. Otherwise prefer the local patch and keep closing.
- Once the current draft, invariant, and short recheck still agree, finalize the solution.

Output only the final Python code enclosed in a code block.

Pseudo:
read N, items
total=sum b
if total%3: print(-1); return
T=total//3
W=T+1
INF=10**9
dp = [[INF]*W for _ in range(W)]
dp[0][0]=0
P=0
for a,b in items:
    ndp = [[INF]*W for _ in range(W)]
    P2 = P + b
    # x range for old states? We loop old states valid for P.
    xl = max(0, P - 2*T)
    xu = min(T, P)
    for x in range(xl, xu+1):
        row_dp = dp[x]
        # y range old valid: y between max(0, P-T-x) and min(T, P-x)
        yl = max(0, P - T - x)
        yu = min(T, P - x)
        if yl > yu: continue
        for y in range(yl, yu+1):
            c = row_dp[y]
            if c == INF: continue
            # team1
            nx = x + b
            if nx <= T:
                nc = c + (a != 1)
                if nc < ndp[nx][y]: ndp[nx][y] = nc
            # team2
            ny = y + b
            if ny <= T:
                nc = c + (a != 2)
                if nc < ndp[ny? wait ndp[x][ny]]
            # team3
            # check new s3 <= T: P2 - x - y <= T => x+y >= P2 - T
            if x + y >= P2 - T:
                nc = c + (a != 3)
                if nc < ndp[x][y]: ndp[x][y] = nc
    dp = ndp
    P = P2
ans = dp[T][T]
print(ans if ans<INF else -1)

Need ensure old state valid: s3 = P - x - y between 0 and T. yl = max(0, P-T-x), yu = min(T, P-x). But also x lower/upper. If P can be >T, x upper min(T,P)=T. If P can be >2T, x lower P-2T. Good. If P < T, x lower 0, upper P (since x<=P). y upper P-x. Good.

Now, is it safe to prune old states with s3>T? Yes. But what about old states with s3 maybe >T that could later be "balanced" by assigning future items to team1/2? No, s3 cannot decrease. We argued. So safe.

But wait: The DP assigns each person to a final team. If a person initially in team3 and assigned to team3, they contribute to s3. If assigned to team1/2, they don't. s3 is final team3 sum. It indeed monotonically increases as we process items. So pruning s3>T is safe.

Now, do we need to track s3 for team3 transition? We check new s3<=T. If old s3<=T, new s3 = old_s3 + b. If >T skip. Good.

But there is another subtlety: We cap s1 and s2 at T. Suppose during processing, s1 >T but later maybe no, final team1 sum cannot decrease, so impossible. Safe.

Now, complexity of scanning old valid states. Need compute P after each item. But P is sum of processed strengths. We process items in input order. Does order matter? No, DP over items with final assignments independent of order. We can process in any order. But pruning s3<=T uses processed sum; order affects which states pruned? Since s3 is sum of weights assigned to team3 among processed items. If we process in different order, a prefix state with s3>T could be pruned; but if final assignment has s3=T, no prefix can have s3>T regardless of order? Wait s3 among processed items depends on which items assigned to team3 and order. Since final team3 sum is T. For any subset of items assigned to team3, its sum <=T. If we process items in arbitrary order, the cumulative sum of team3-assigned items among processed items is sum of some subset of final team3 items; since all weights positive and total team3 sum T, any partial sum of that subset <=T. Thus no prefix s3>T. So pruning safe for any order. Similarly s1/s2 partial sums <=T. Good.

Now, can we further optimize by grouping items? Maybe not needed.

Implementation:
import sys
def main():
    data = list(map(int, sys.stdin.buffer.read().split()))
    if not data: return
    N = data[0]
    A = []
    B = []
    idx=1
    total=0
    items=[]
    for _ in range(N):
        a=data[idx]; b=data[idx+1]; idx+=2
        items.append((a,b))
        total += b
    if total % 3 != 0:
        print(-1); return
    T = total // 3
    W = T + 1
    INF = 10**9
    dp = [[INF] * W for _ in range(W)]
    dp[0][0] = 0
    P = 0
    for a,b in items:
        ndp = [[INF] * W for _ in range(W)]
        P2 = P + b
        # old valid x range
        xl = P - 2*T
        if xl < 0: xl = 0
        xu = P
        if xu > T: xu = T
        # cost offsets
        cost1 = 0 if a == 1 else 1
        cost2 = 0 if a == 2 else 1
        cost3 = 0 if a == 3 else 1
        # maybe if xl > xu? Could happen? P maybe? P<=total<=3T, xl<=xu always? Check P=0 xl=0 xu=0. P=3T xl=T xu=T. Good.
        for x in range(xl, xu + 1):
            row = dp[x]
            # y lower/upper for old state
            yl = P - T - x
            if yl < 0: yl = 0
            yu = P - x
            if yu > T: yu = T
            if yl > yu:
                continue
            # For team2, need y+b <= T. We can set y_limit = T - b. But y range maybe.
            # For team1, need x+b <=T. Since x up to T; if x > T-b skip team1.
            can1 = x + b <= T
            # team3 condition uses x+y >= P2 - T
            min_xy = P2 - T
            # We'll loop y.
            for y in range(yl, yu + 1):
                c = row[y]
                if c == INF:
                    continue
                if can1:
                    nc = c + cost1
                    if nc < ndp[x + b][y]:
                        ndp[x + b][y] = nc
                if y + b <= T:
                    nc = c + cost2
                    if nc < ndp[x][y + b]:
                        ndp[x][y + b] = nc
                if x + y >= min_xy:
                    nc = c + cost3
                    if nc < ndp[x][y]:
                        ndp[x][y] = nc
        dp = ndp
        P = P2
    ans = dp[T][T]
    print(ans if ans < INF else -1)
if __name__ == "__main__": main()

Need check `can1` computed outside y loop. If x+b<=T. Good. But if b large, can1 false for many x. Team2 check inside y. Could compute `can2_y_limit = T - b`; if y <= T-b. Since y range maybe. Use `if y <= T - b:` to avoid addition each time? Addition cheap. Could precompute `yb_limit = T - b`. Then `if y <= yb_limit`. But if b>T, yb_limit negative, no team2. Similarly can1 x <= T-b. We'll use.

For team3 condition `x + y >= min_xy`. If min_xy <=0, always true. We can compute. Note if min_xy > 2T, no state satisfies? P2<=3T => min_xy<=2T. Good.

Potential issue: We prune old states with s3<=T. But our y range `yu = min(T, P - x)` ensures s3>=0? s3 = P - x - y >=0 => y <= P-x. Yes. yl ensures s3<=T. Good. But what about x lower/upper? xl = max(0, P-2T) ensures there exists y<=T with y>=P-T-x. Let's verify if xl = P-2T (if positive). If x = P-2T, then P-T-x = T, yl=T, yu=min(T,P-x). P-x = 2T? Wait x=P-2T => P-x=2T, yu=T, so y=T valid. If x < P-2T, P-T-x > T, yl>T, yu<=T, no valid. So xl correct. xu = min(T,P) ensures y>=0 possible. Good.

Let's write optimized inner loop:
for x in range(xl, xu + 1):
    row = dp[x]
    ndp_x = ndp[x]
    yl = P - T - x
    if yl < 0: yl = 0
    yu = P - x
    if yu > T: yu = T
    if yl > yu: continue
    can1 = x + b <= T
    row_nx = ndp[x + b] if can1 else None
    yb_limit = T - b
    # But yb_limit same for all x; compute outside x loop.
    for y in range(yl, yu + 1):
        c = row[y]
        if c == INF:
            continue
        if can1:
            nc = c + cost1
            if nc < row_nx[y]:
                row_nx[y] = nc
        if y <= yb_limit:
            nc = c + cost2
            if nc < ndp_x[y + b]:
                ndp_x[y + b] = nc
        if x + y >= min_xy:
            nc = c + cost3
            if nc < ndp_x[y]:
                ndp_x[y] = nc

Now, let's think about if we can use "dp over costs" bitset to be faster and maybe code shorter? Let's explore bitset approach in case current DP might TLE. But current likely okay. Still, let's examine bitset for robustness.

Bitset DP idea:
State index = x*W + y. W=T+1.
For each cost c, bitset reachable states after processing some items with exactly c switches. Initially bits[0] has bit 0.
For each item (a,b):
- We need compute new bits from old bits. Since cost 0 for same team, cost 1 for other two.
Let old_bits = bits (list of ints length N+1).
For each c where old_bits[c] nonzero:
  same_team_shift:
    if a==1: shift by b*W (team1) with mask rows x<=T-b
    if a==2: shift by b (team2) with mask columns y<=T-b
    if a==3: shift 0 (team3) with mask s3<=T? Wait same team for a=3 means assign to team3, state index unchanged, but need new s3<=T. Since old s3<=T? If we maintain only valid states with s3<=T, assigning to team3 may violate. Need mask. For a=1 same team team1: state index shifts by b*W, s3 unchanged, so if old valid, new valid. Need x+b<=T. For a=2 shift by b, need y+b<=T. For a=3 shift 0, need new s3 = old_s3+b <=T, i.e. old state must satisfy P - x - y + b <=T. This is not just a simple bit shift; need mask by states with x+y >= P+b-T. Could apply mask.
  other teams shifts similarly.
But maintaining bitsets per cost and masks for each P/b is complex. Also need exact cost. Not worth.

Another bitset approach: For each possible cost, maintain bitset of (x,y) and use shifts with masks. Could be fast but code more complex. Current DP simpler.

If multiple switches were allowed, a person could switch from team1 to team2 to team3, costing 2, but they could instead switch directly from team1 to team3 costing 1. Can we use bitset for transitions to be faster? Let's see if we can implement a correct bitset DP with masks maybe not too bad.

Bitset DP idea more detailed:
We need maintain for each cost c a bitset of states (x,y) reachable. State index = x*W + y. W=T+1, total states M=W*W <=251001 bits ~31KB. Costs 0..N (100). Total bitsets 101 * 31KB =3.1MB. Operations: For each item, for each cost c from 0..i, compute new bits for cost c and c+1. But we can update in place descending cost? Let's derive.

Let bits[c] = reachable states after processed items with exactly c switches. Initially bits[0]=1<<0.

For each item (a,b):
We need produce new_bits. For each old cost c:
- Assign to same team as a: cost 0. New state = shift depending on team:
  - if a==1: shift by b*W (team1) with mask row x<=T-b.
  - if a==2: shift by b (team2) with mask col y<=T-b.
  - if a==3: shift 0 (team3) but need new s3<=T. Since old state valid s3<=T? If bitsets only contain valid states for current P, then assigning to team3 may violate. Need mask by states with old s3 + b <= T. This is not a simple shift; it's a subset mask depending on P and b. We can apply mask to old bits before shift 0.
- Assign to other teams: cost 1. Similar shifts.

Because cost 0/1, we can update:
new_bits[c] |= shift_same(old_bits[c])
new_bits[c+1] |= shift_other1(old_bits[c]) | shift_other2(old_bits[c])
But if we update in-place descending c, need be careful because new_bits[c] also receives from old_bits[c-1] via other teams. If we iterate c descending, old_bits[c-1] not yet updated? Let's see:
For c from i down to 0:
  # add same-team transitions from old c to new c
  bits[c] = (bits[c] shifted same) | (bits[c-1] shifted other) ??? But bits[c] currently old; if we overwrite, we lose old for lower c? Descending ensures when computing c, bits[c-1] is still old (not yet overwritten), and bits[c] old can be overwritten. But bits[c] also receives from old bits[c] same; we can compute new_bits[c] = old_bits[c] shifted same | old_bits[c-1] shifted other. Then assign bits[c] = new_bits[c]. For c=0, no old_bits[-1]. For c=i+1, bits[i+1] currently 0 (or old from previous? Actually max cost increases by1, so bits[i+1] should be 0 before; but if we iterate descending, we need set bits[i+1] from old_bits[i] other. We can handle by starting c from i+1 down to0? Let's formulate.

Let old = bits copy? In-place descending:
for c in range(i, -1, -1):
    old_c = bits[c]  # but if bits[c] has been overwritten? Descending: when c=i, bits[i] old; bits[i+1] maybe old 0. We compute new_i = shift_same(old_i) | (shift_other(old_{i-1}) if c>0). But old_{i-1} not overwritten yet. Then assign bits[i] = new_i.
    # Need also compute bits[i+1] from old_i other. If we assign bits[i+1] before overwriting bits[i]? We can compute new_{i+1} = shift_other(old_i) | (maybe shift_same old_{i+1}? old_{i+1}=0). Then assign bits[i+1].
But if we assign bits[i+1] at iteration c=i, and later at iteration c=i+1? We don't iterate i+1 if range i..0. So okay.
For c=i-1, bits[i-1] old; bits[i] now new, but we need old_i for computing bits[i+1]? Already done. Need old_{i-1} for bits[i]? Already done. Need old_{i-1} for bits[i]? Wait new_i also receives from old_{i-1} other. At iteration c=i, we need old_{i-1} to compute new_i. We can access bits[i-1] (old) because not overwritten yet. Good. Then assign bits[i]. At iteration c=i-1, we compute new_{i-1} = shift_same(old_{i-1}) | shift_other(old_{i-2}). But bits[i-1] currently old? We didn't overwrite it yet? Actually we assigned bits[i] at c=i, not bits[i-1]. So bits[i-1] old. Good. Then assign bits[i-1]. At c=i-1, also need compute new_i from old_{i-1} other? But new_i already computed at c=i using old_{i-1}. So don't recompute. Thus in-place descending works if at each c we compute:
new_c = shift_same(old_c) | (shift_other(old_{c-1}) if c>0 else 0)
new_{c+1} = shift_other(old_c)  # but if c+1 has already been assigned? At c=i, c+1=i+1 not assigned yet. At c=i-1, c+1=i was assigned at previous iteration; we must OR into it, not overwrite. So we need `bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)`.
But if we do `bits[c] = new_c`, and `bits[c+1] |= ...`, need ensure when c=i-1, bits[i] currently new_i; OR shift_other(old_{i-1}) into bits[i]. That's correct because new_i should include old_{i-1} other. Wait at c=i, we computed new_i = shift_same(old_i) | shift_other(old_{i-1})? We included old_{i-1} other at c=i. Then at c=i-1, we would OR shift_other(old_{i-1}) into bits[i] again, duplicate but OR idempotent. But if we didn't include at c=i, we can include at c=i-1. In-place descending with two contributions:
For c from i down to 0:
    old_c = bits[c]  # before overwrite
    # same-team transition from old_c to c
    bits[c] = shift_same(old_c)
    # other-team transition from old_c to c+1
    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)

But wait, when c=i, bits[i+1] |= shift_other(old_i) works. bits[i] = shift_same(old_i); then bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1}) (if c>0). Good.
When c=i-1, bits[i-1] = shift_same(old_{i-1}); bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1}) (again, duplicate). But bits[i] already got shift_other(old_{i-1}) from previous c=i? Actually previous c=i did `bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1})`? Wait previous c=i, c>0, bits[i] |= shift_other(bits[i-1]) where bits[i-1] old_{i-1}. So yes, bits[i] already includes old_{i-1} other. Then at c=i-1, `bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1})` duplicate. We can avoid by not doing the `if c>0` at c=i? Hmm.

In-place descending with careful duplicate okay but OR idempotent, so duplicate not incorrect, just extra work. We can avoid duplicate by computing new_c at c from shift_same(old_c) | (shift_other(old_{c-1}) if c>0 else 0) and bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c). But if at c we compute new_c including old_{c-1}, then when we later reach c-1, we should not OR old_{c-1} into bits[c] again. So do not have `if c>0` separately. Let's write:

    old_c = bits[c]
    # compute new for cost c: same from old_c, plus other from old_{c-1} (if any)

    if c > 0:
        val |= shift_other(bits[c-1])  # bits[c-1] old because descending
    bits[c] = val

    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)
But note when c=i, bits[i+1] initially 0 (or old? max cost before item i is i, so bits[i+1] should be 0). We OR shift_other(old_i). Good.
When c=i-1, bits[i] currently new_i (set at c=i). We OR shift_other(old_{i-1}) into bits[i]. This is needed because new_i should include old_{i-1} other. But wait at c=i, we computed val = shift_same(old_i) | shift_other(bits[i-1]) (old_{i-1}) and set bits[i]=val. So bits[i] already includes old_{i-1} other. Then at c=i-1, ORing shift_other(old_{i-1}) into bits[i] is duplicate. So we should not include old_{c-1} at c=i if we will include at c=i-1. Better:
for c in range(i, -1, -1):
    old_c = bits[c]
    bits[c] = shift_same(old_c)  # overwrite with same-team from old_c
    if c > 0:
        bits[c] |= shift_other(bits[c-1])  # other from old_{c-1}; bits[c-1] old
    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)  # other from old_c to c+1
But when c=i, bits[i] = shift_same(old_i); bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1}); bits[i+1] |= shift_other(old_i). Good.
When c=i-1, bits[i-1] = shift_same(old_{i-1}); bits[i-1] |= shift_other(old_{i-2}); bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1}) (duplicate, but OR idempotent). So duplicate only once per cost, not incorrect. Extra OR operations. We can avoid by not doing `bits[c] |= shift_other(bits[c-1])` at c, and instead when computing bits[c] at c, include old_{c-1}? That's what we did first. Duplicate either way. To avoid duplicate, need use old_bits copy or more complex. Duplicate OR is okay; number of shifts doubles? Actually each old cost's other transition is ORed twice? Let's see: old_{c-1} other is ORed into bits[c] at iteration c (via `bits[c] |= shift_other(bits[c-1])`) and at iteration c-1 (via `bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})`). So yes, each other transition computed twice. That doubles shift operations. We can avoid by choosing one method.

Method A:
for c in range(i, -1, -1):
    old_c = bits[c]
    val = shift_same(old_c)
    if c > 0:
        val |= shift_other(bits[c-1])  # bits[c-1] old
    bits[c] = val
    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)
Here old_{c-1} other is used in val at iteration c, and old_{c-1} other is also used at iteration c-1 to OR into bits[c]? Wait at iteration c-1, `bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})` happens because c-1's old_c = old_{c-1}, and `bits[(c-1)+1] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})` -> bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1}). So duplicate. To avoid, in iteration c-1, we should not OR into bits[c] if we already included at iteration c. But we need OR into bits[c] for old_{c-1} other. If we included at iteration c, then at iteration c-1 we can skip the `bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)` for c-1? But that same operation also needed for old_{c-1} to cost c. We already did it at iteration c. So we can skip `bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)` for all c? No, for old_i to cost i+1, no later iteration will do it. Let's define:
At iteration c, we need:
- same from old_c to cost c.
- other from old_c to cost c+1.
- other from old_{c-1} to cost c (if c>0).
If we process descending, when at c, old_c is available, old_{c-1} is available. We can compute:
bits[c] = shift_same(old_c) | (shift_other(old_{c-1}) if c>0 else 0)
bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)
Then at iteration c-1, we will compute bits[c-1] = shift_same(old_{c-1}) | (shift_other(old_{c-2}) if c-1>0 else 0)
bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})  # This duplicates the other from old_{c-1} to cost c that was already included in bits[c] at iteration c.
So duplicate. To avoid, at iteration c, when computing bits[c], do NOT include other from old_{c-1}; instead, that contribution will be added at iteration c-1 via `bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})`. Then:
At iteration c:
    old_c = bits[c]
    bits[c] = shift_same(old_c)  # overwrite, losing old_c but saved
    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)
    # Do not add old_{c-1} yet.
At iteration c-1:
    old_{c-1} = bits[c-1] (old)
    bits[c-1] = shift_same(old_{c-1})
    bits[c] |= shift_other(old_{c-1})  # add other from old_{c-1} to cost c
    bits[c] currently new from same old_c plus maybe other from old_{c-1}? Wait bits[c] was set at iteration c to shift_same(old_c). At iteration c-1, we OR shift_other(old_{c-1}) into bits[c]. This gives new_c = shift_same(old_c) | shift_other(old_{c-1}). Correct. No duplicate.
But what about other from old_{c-1} to cost c also needs to be added to bits[c] before we use bits[c] for anything? We don't use bits[c] as old in later iterations because descending; later iterations are c-2, c-3, which don't read bits[c] except when adding other from old_{c-2} to cost c-1? They read bits[c-1], not bits[c]. So order okay.
At the end, after loop c=0, bits[1] has been ORed with shift_other(old_0) at iteration 0. But bits[1] was also set at iteration 1 to shift_same(old_1) and ORed with shift_other(old_0) at iteration 0? Wait iteration 1: bits[1] = shift_same(old_1); bits[2] |= shift_other(old_1). Then iteration 0: bits[0] = shift_same(old_0); bits[1] |= shift_other(old_0). So bits[1] = shift_same(old_1) | shift_other(old_0). Correct. No duplicate. Good.
But what about old_0 other to cost1: added at iteration0. Good.
What about old_i other to cost i+1: added at iteration i. Good.
What about old_{c-1} other to cost c: added at iteration c-1, not at iteration c. Good.

So in-place descending algorithm:
for c in range(i, -1, -1):
    old_c = bits[c]
    bits[c] = shift_same(old_c)  # same-team from old_c to c
    bits[c+1] |= shift_other(old_c)  # other-team from old_c to c+1
    # Wait, if we set bits[c] = shift_same(old_c), we overwrite old_c. But we saved old_c. Good.
    # But what about other from old_{c-1} to c? It will be added when c-1 iteration does bits[(c-1)+1] |= shift_other(old_{c-1}). So after loop, bits[c] includes it. Good.
But need ensure when c=i, bits[i+1] initially 0; OR shift_other(old_i). Good.
When c=i-1, bits[i] currently shift_same(old_i) (set at c=i). Then at c=i-1, bits[i] |= shift_other(old_{i-1}). So bits[i] becomes shift_same(old_i) | shift_other(old_{i-1}). Correct.
When c=i-2, bits[i-1] currently shift_same(old_{i-1}) (set at c=i-1). At c=i-2, bits[i-1] |= shift_other(old_{i-2}). So bits[i-1] becomes shift_same(old_{i-1}) | shift_other(old_{i-2}). Correct.
But wait, bits[i-1] should also include other from old_i? No, old_i other goes to cost i+1, not i-1. Same from old_i goes to i. So no.
Thus algorithm works.

Need define shift_same and shift_other for a given item. They are bit shifts with masks.

Let's define state index = x*W + y. We need masks:
- Row mask for team1: allow x <= T-b. We can create a bitmask `mask_row_limit` with bits for states with x <= T-b. Then shift by b*W. But shifting old bits by b*W may bring bits from rows x > T-b into valid rows? Actually if old state has x > T-b, x+b > T, invalid. We should mask old bits to x<=T-b before shifting. So `shift_team1(bits) = (bits & mask_x_le[T-b]) << (b*W)`.
- Column mask for team2: allow y <= T-b. `shift_team2(bits) = (bits & mask_y_le[T-b]) << b`.
- Team3: state index unchanged, but need old s3 + b <= T. Since old s3 = P - x - y. Condition P - x - y + b <= T => x+y >= P + b - T = P2 - T. So mask `mask_s3_after_team3` = states with x+y >= P2 - T. Also old state must be valid (s3<=T) but bitsets should only contain valid states if we maintain. We can apply mask. `shift_team3(bits) = bits & mask_ge[P2 - T]` (where mask_ge[k] = states with x+y >= k). If k<=0, all states. If k>2T, none.
- But also team3 assignment doesn't change x,y, so no shift.

However, bitsets may contain states with s3>T? We should maintain only valid states. The shifts team1/team2 preserve s3, so if old bits valid, new bits valid. Team3 shift applies mask to ensure new s3<=T. But what about old bits containing invalid states? We should ensure not. Initially valid. Team1/team2 from valid old states produce valid new states (s3 unchanged). Team3 from valid old states with mask produce valid. So bitsets remain valid. Good.

Need also ensure team1/team2 new x/y <=T. Mask before shift handles. But after shift, could bits wrap from rows? If old x<=T-b, shifting by b*W maps to row x+b <=T. No wrap because x+b<=T. Since W = T+1, row index x+b <=T, so index (x+b)*W+y <= T*W+T = W^2-1. Good. If old x>T-b, masked out. Good.
Team2 shift by b: old y<=T-b, new y+b<=T. But shifting a bit by b can wrap from end of row to next row if old y > T-b. Masked out. Good.

Need masks:
- `mask_x_le[L]`: bits for states with x <= L. Since index = x*W+y. We can build as sum over x=0..L of bits for row x: all y 0..T. Row mask = ((1 << W) - 1) << (x*W). So mask_x_le[L] = OR rows 0..L.
- `mask_y_le[L]`: bits for states with y <= L. This is periodic every W bits: for each row, bits y=0..L. Could build by iterating x=0..T: row_mask_y = (1 << (L+1)) - 1; mask |= row_mask_y << (x*W). Since L <=T.
- `mask_ge[K]`: states with x+y >= K. Could build by iterating x,y and setting bit if x+y>=K. But M=251k, building masks maybe okay. Need masks for various K values (P2-T) for each item, up to 100. We can precompute all mask_ge[0..2T]? That would be 1001 masks * 31KB =31MB, maybe okay but large. Or compute on the fly? Building mask_ge for each item by iterating 251k bits would be 25M operations, okay but maybe similar to DP. But bitset DP already complex.

Alternatively, for team3, we can avoid mask by using a different encoding? Not.

Given complexity, current DP is better for final answer.

Let's not pursue bitset.

Could optimize current DP by using active list and avoiding scanning invalid states? Let's see if we can implement active list efficiently with 2D array and list of keys, and build new active by scanning ndp valid ranges. This might reduce scanning if many INF, but not transitions. It adds code. Current code is simpler.
